| 1395 | verse-reading | draft | In Isa.38.18 H3034 is negated and attributed to Sheol/death as impossible subjects -- the surface sense shifts from an act performed by the living toward a rhetorical impossibility, underscoring that thanksgiving requires life and consciousness; this negative framing is a distinct nuance compared to the affirmative liturgical occurrences elsewhere. | Isa.38.18 |
| 1420 | verse-reading | draft | M08/M16/M22 (praise) and M68 (hope) and M13 (faithfulness) co-occur; H3034 is negated in parallel with praise (M22), both impossible for the dead, while hope (M68) and faithfulness (M13) belong to a distinct clause about the living seeking God -- H3034 relates to praise as paired negated impossibility, not to hope/faithfulness directly. | Isa.38.18 |
| 1793 | verse-reading | draft | In Isa.38.18 the verb is negated and applied to Sheol/death as impossible subjects ('Sheol does not thank you') -- this pushes the term into a rhetorical argument about the incapacity of the dead to praise, a notable contextual application distinguishing it from the cluster's typical positive liturgical occurrences elsewhere. | Isa.38.18 |
| 1801 | verse-reading | draft | No role-T3 tag on H3034 in Isa.38.18; the operation-tagged word here is H3381 'go down' (T3), initiated by 'those who go down to the pit' toward the pit itself -- unrelated to H3034 which is negated and not tagged T3. | Isa.38.18 |
| 1810 | verse-reading | draft | Building on the prior finding that H3034 pairs with negated praise (M22) while hope (M68) and faithfulness (M13) belong to a separate clause: from H3034's own vantage, its negation functions rhetorically to argue from the incapacity of the dead to the urgency of thanksgiving among the living -- the same logic Hezekiah applies to himself elsewhere in the psalm. | Isa.38.18 |
| 2211 | verse-reading | draft | In Lev.16.21 'confess' renders the Hithpael sense (confession of sin transferred onto the scapegoat), a distinct nuance from the Hiphil 'give thanks/praise' sense seen elsewhere in this batch (e.g. Ps.7.17, Ps.18.49) -- here the impact is ritual/transferential rather than doxological, marking the negative/penitential pole of the same root. | Lev.16.21 |
| 2215 | verse-reading | draft | No other M-code characteristic co-occurs with H3034 in Lev.16.21; the confession act stands alone amid role-only tags (M-codes present belong to non-home strongs but none are flagged as sharing this cluster's relational chain in prior context, and no prior pass exists for this verse). | Lev.16.21 |
| 2381 | verse-reading | draft | In Ps.88.10, H3034 (praise) is posed rhetorically as something the dead CANNOT do; M48 (wonders) and M55 (departed/shades) are present but the relation is negative/hypothetical -- praise is denied to the realm of the dead, contrasting rather than reinforcing the other M-codes present. | Ps.88.10 |
| 2433 | verse-reading | draft | In Ps.49.18 the Hophal 'praise' (yehullu) is used in a sarcastic, worldly context -- people 'praise' the self-satisfied rich man rather than God -- diverging from H3034's usual cultic-worship object; the impact is to expose empty human flattery as a hollow counterfeit of true praise-to-God, contrasting with the term's normal divine-directed occurrences elsewhere in the Psalter. | Ps.49.18 |
| 2439 | verse-reading | draft | In Ps.49.18 H3034 (praise, of the flattering crowd) co-occurs with M47 (soul/self), M25/M44 (life), M80 (blessed), M05 (do well); but this 'praise' is ironic human flattery of the self-satisfied man, not related to the other M-codes' portrayal of blessedness or life -- it stands apart as the counterfeit response critiqued by the passage. | Ps.49.18 |
| 2544 | verse-reading | draft | In Isa 38:19 'the living...he thanks you' places the thanksgiving specifically in the mouth of the living (as opposed to the dead in Sheol, per the surrounding context), foregrounding the base gloss's sense but adding a contrastive nuance -- thanks as the unique prerogative and duty of the living, distinguishing it from typical worship-refrain occurrences like the Psalms usages above. | Isa.38.19 |
| 2550 | verse-reading | draft | In Isa 38:19, other M-codes present are M25/M44 (the living), M15 (making known), and M13 (faithfulness); thanksgiving here is the act performed uniquely by the living, paired with the father's making known God's faithfulness to children -- thanksgiving and making-known-faithfulness form a parallel transmission structure (praise now, teaching for the next generation) without one being the source of the other. | Isa.38.19 |
| 2566 | verse-reading | draft | In Rev 4:9, thanks is grouped with glory and honor as part of the living creatures' worship formula, sitting within the base gloss of thanksgiving as an element of doxological praise, matching typical NT worship-scene usage. | Rev.4.9 |
| 2569 | verse-reading | draft | In Rev 4:9, other M-codes present are M25 (living creatures), M22 (glory), M71 (honor); thanksgiving stands alongside glory and honor as one of three coordinate objects offered in worship to the one on the throne, functioning as a parallel, non-hierarchical companion term to glory and honor rather than derived from or causing them. | Rev.4.9 |
| 2570 | verse-reading | draft | In Rev 4:9 the operation-tagged word 'give' (G1325) is initiated by the living creatures (party) and directed toward 'him who is seated on the throne' -- thanks (alongside glory and honor) is the object given, flowing from the worshiping creatures to God as recipient. | Rev.4.9 |
| 2824 | verse-reading | draft | Here the verb appears in a rhetorical question denying the very possibility of praise from the dead ('in Sheol who will give you praise?'); rather than describing praise being offered, it names praise's absence/impossibility -- a negative-conditional use distinct from the affirmative thanksgiving sense found in Ps.138.1, Ps.33.2, Ps.57.9. The impact is theological: it grounds an appeal for deliverance in the premise that only the living can render this praise. | Ps.6.5 |
| 2825 | verse-reading | draft | No other M-code characteristic co-occurs in this verse; 'remembrance' (H2143) and 'death'/'Sheol' terms are tagged T-codes only, not M-codes, so thanksgiving/praise stands as the verse's sole characteristic, here negated. | Ps.6.5 |
| 2826 | verse-reading | draft | No role-T3 Operation tag is present in this verse's role list at all; the verb H3034 itself carries no role-T3 tag here, so no operation-party relation is recorded. | Ps.6.5 |
| 2911 | verse-reading | draft | In Lam.3.53 H3034 Piel is rendered 'cast' (cast stones) -- this reflects the older, literal root sense of throwing/casting rather than the more frequent Hiphil sense 'give thanks/praise'; a marked divergence in this occurrence compared to the cluster's typical worship-related uses. | Lam.3.53 |
| 2912 | verse-reading | draft | H3034 (cast, Piel) is the operation here, initiated by unnamed enemies/persecutors ('they') and directed toward the speaker ('me'), a hostile physical act (casting stones), not thanksgiving. | Lam.3.53 |
| 2913 | verse-reading | draft | M10 (flung), M25/M44 (alive) also occur here; from thanksgiving's own vantage, in this occurrence H3034 does not carry its thanksgiving sense at all but its literal 'casting' root sense, so it stands unrelated in meaning to any thanksgiving-theme co-occurrence -- purely coincidental lexical overlap of the same root. | Lam.3.53 |
| 2963 | char-reading | draft | G2169 is the sole member strong of this subgroup, so there is no cross-strong divergence to compare -- every occurrence uniformly names the noun-form state/act of thanksgiving (gratitude, the giving of thanks, or a liturgical category of thanks), never a different root sense; the subgroup is internally homogeneous at the lexical level, with variation occurring only in referent (God, a human patron) and rhetorical role (doxological list item, prayer accompaniment, virtue-marker, produced overflow). | 1Cor.14.16, Rev.7.12, 2Cor.9.12, Eph.5.4, Phil.4.6, 1Tim.2.1, 1Thess.3.9, Col.4.2 … |
| 2965 | char-reading | draft | Across its full occurrence list, G2169 clusters into three recognizable functional groups sharing genuinely alike readings: (1) doxological/liturgical enumeration where thanksgiving is one item in a fixed list of praise-terms offered to God (Rev.7.12; Rev.4.9); (2) prayer-accompaniment usage where thanksgiving qualifies or accompanies the manner of praying/receiving (Phil.4.6; Col.4.2; 1Tim.2.1; 1Tim.4.3; 1Tim.4.4; Col.2.7); (3) thanksgiving as the relational/social outcome or debt produced by another's action -- generosity, grace, ministry, or joy (2Cor.9.11; 2Cor.9.12; 2Cor.4.15; 1Thess.3.9). Acts.24.3 and Eph.5.4 stand somewhat apart as non-liturgical, interpersonal/ethical uses (gratitude toward a civic patron; grateful speech replacing crude talk) and 1Cor.14.16 stands apart as a specifically public, intelligibility-conditioned liturgical speech-act. | Rev.7.12, Rev.4.9, Phil.4.6, Col.4.2, 1Tim.2.1, 1Tim.4.3, 1Tim.4.4, Col.2.7 … |
| 3034 | char-answers | draft | None recorded for the remaining occurrences (doxological, prayer-accompaniment, and outcome usages) -- these do not disclose a specific perception/belief/judgement content of the person expressing thanksgiving beyond the general recognition of a benefit received; the noun names the state/act rather than articulating a propositional judgement. | Rev.7.12, Rev.4.9, 2Cor.9.11, 2Cor.9.12, 2Cor.4.15, Phil.4.6, Col.4.2, 1Tim.2.1 … |
| 3037 | char-answers | draft | Rev.7.12 and Rev.4.9 place thanksgiving within eternal doxology ('forever and ever'), showing an orientation toward an eschatological fullness in which thanksgiving is the permanent, unceasing state of the redeemed and living creatures before God's throne. | Rev.7.12, Rev.4.9 |
| 3041 | char-answers | draft | Yes, the mechanism differs by context: in 2Cor.9.11-12 thanksgiving is produced mechanically through generosity administered by ministry ('overflowing... produce thanksgiving'); in 1Tim.4.3-4 it is the mode of right reception of food/creation; in Phil.4.6/Col.4.2 it is the accompanying manner of prayer; in Eph.5.4 it is a replacement mode of speech; in Rev.7.12/Rev.4.9 it is doxological utterance in worship. | 2Cor.9.11, 2Cor.9.12, 1Tim.4.3, 1Tim.4.4, Phil.4.6, Col.4.2, Eph.5.4, Rev.7.12 … |
| 3042 | char-answers | draft | Thanksgiving's role reads as belonging to created design: it is the fitting response to created goodness (1Tim.4.3-4, 'everything created by God is good'), to grace received, and to the eternal worship of unfallen/glorified beings (Rev.4.9, Rev.7.12) -- nothing in the evidence ties it to the fallen condition, though its absence (replaced by foolish talk in Eph.5.4) implicates the fallen condition negatively. | 1Tim.4.3, 1Tim.4.4, Rev.4.9, Rev.7.12, Eph.5.4 |
| 3045 | char-answers | draft | None recorded for the remaining occurrences -- most instances (doxological lists, prayer-accompaniment formulas, outcome statements) do not specify felt/bodily quality, presenting thanksgiving as a named category or act without emotion-language attached. | Rev.7.12, Rev.4.9, 2Cor.9.11, 2Cor.9.12, 2Cor.4.15, Phil.4.6, 1Tim.2.1, 1Tim.4.3 … |
| 3047 | char-answers | draft | The mode of operation varies by context: in public worship it is spoken/liturgical utterance requiring communal intelligibility (1Cor.14.16); in personal ethics it is a mode of speech replacing crude talk (Eph.5.4); in devotional life it accompanies prayer (Phil.4.6; Col.4.2; 1Tim.2.1); in reception of gifts it is the manner of receiving (1Tim.4.3-4); in ministry it is the overflow-result of generosity (2Cor.9.11-12); in eternal worship it is doxological ascription (Rev.4.9; Rev.7.12); toward a human benefactor it is civic gratitude (Acts.24.3). | 1Cor.14.16, Eph.5.4, Phil.4.6, Col.4.2, 1Tim.2.1, 1Tim.4.3, 1Tim.4.4, 2Cor.9.11 … |
| 3048 | char-answers | draft | Yes: G2169 repeatedly operates through speech -- as public prayer-speech requiring hearer comprehension and assent (1Cor.14.16 'say Amen'), as doxological proclamation (Rev.4.9 'give... thanks,' Rev.7.12 'saying'), and as a mode of everyday conversation contrasted with crude talk (Eph.5.4). | 1Cor.14.16, Rev.4.9, Rev.7.12, Eph.5.4 |
| 3059 | char-answers | draft | Thanksgiving is an exclusively creaturely analogue rather than a quality shared identically with God: God is consistently the recipient/object of thanksgiving in this evidence (Rev.4.9; Rev.7.12; 2Cor.4.15, etc.), never portrayed as himself giving thanks -- the characteristic belongs structurally to the creature's posture toward the Creator. | Rev.4.9, Rev.7.12 |
| 3061 | char-answers | draft | The evidence engages spirit-level ('give thanks with your spirit,' 1Cor.14.16) and the domain of speech/mouth (Eph.5.4's contrast with crude talk; Rev.4.9's 'saying'), showing thanksgiving's seat spans spirit and speech-organ expression; it never engages purely bodily/physical sensation language, indicating its depth is in the spirit/mind with the body (mouth/speech) as an expressive, not merely functional, link. | 1Cor.14.16, Eph.5.4, Rev.4.9 |
| 3071 | char-answers | draft | No occurrence in this subgroup's own evidence shows thanksgiving being communicated, strengthened, or mediated through angelic ministry; the doxological scenes (Rev.4.9; Rev.7.12) show living creatures and heavenly beings themselves offering thanksgiving to God, not mediating it to humans. | Rev.4.9, Rev.7.12 |
| 3074 | char-answers | draft | G2169 names a compound inner-being phenomenon: primarily a disposition/state (gratitude as settled attitude, e.g. Col.2.7's 'abounding'), also a concrete act (the giving of thanks, e.g. Rev.4.9's 'give... thanks'), and occasionally a countable event/category listed among prayer-types (1Tim.2.1's plural 'thanksgivings'). | Col.2.7, Rev.4.9, 1Tim.2.1 |
| 3079 | char-answers | draft | Thanksgiving most frequently co-occurs with prayer/supplication (Phil.4.6; Col.4.2; 1Tim.2.1), and with glory/honor/power in doxological lists (Rev.4.9; Rev.7.12); also with faith/belief-of-truth (1Tim.4.3; Col.2.7), grace (2Cor.4.15), generosity (2Cor.9.11-12), and joy (1Thess.3.9). | Phil.4.6, Col.4.2, 1Tim.2.1, Rev.4.9, Rev.7.12, 1Tim.4.3, Col.2.7, 2Cor.4.15 … |
| 3086 | char-answers | draft | The closest adjacent characteristic is glory/honor (as in the doxological lists of Rev.4.9 and Rev.7.12); thanksgiving is distinguished from these by its specifically responsive structure -- it names gratitude for benefit received, while glory/honor name recognition of inherent worth/majesty, not necessarily tied to a received gift. | Rev.4.9, Rev.7.12 |
| 3088 | char-answers | draft | The distinction from the nearest neighbour (glory/honor) is one of kind and direction: thanksgiving is a response-category (moving from creature to God in acknowledgment of benefit), while glory/honor are attribution-categories (ascribing inherent worth), not primarily response-shaped. | Rev.4.9 |
| 3202 | char-reading | draft | Two occurrences in this pass ('shoot' in Jer.50.14, Qal, and 'down'/'cast' in Zech.1.21 and Lam.3.53, Piel/Qal) return to the literal, pre-metaphorical root sense of throwing/casting/shooting -- entirely unrelated in these instances to the thanksgiving/confession branch, showing the root's physical origin persisting untransformed in martial and violent contexts (bending the bow to shoot Babylon; casting down the nations' horns; casting stones on the speaker in the pit). | Jer.50.14, Zech.1.21, Lam.3.53 |
| 3203 | char-reading | draft | Within the confession-branch occurrences themselves, a further distinction is visible by stem/subject: Lev.16.21 (Qal-tq, Aaron confessing the people's sin OVER the scapegoat) and Num.5.7/Lev.26.40/Lev.5.5 (individual or corporate confession as a required legal/cultic act tied to restitution or covenant curse) represent a ritual-legal register, whereas Dan.9.20, Neh.1.6, Ezra.10.1, Neh.9.3, Dan.9.4 represent a personal/intercessory-prayer register (confession embedded within extended penitential prayer, weeping, and prostration) -- both are 'confession' but differ in whether the act is a discrete ritual requirement or an extended devotional/intercessory posture. | Lev.16.21, Num.5.7, Lev.26.40, Lev.5.5, Dan.9.20, Neh.1.6, Ezra.10.1, Neh.9.3 … |
| 3214 | char-answers | draft | The transformation from silence/complaint or guilt to thanksgiving/confession is depicted as reversible in principle (a person may cease thanking, as Sheol/the dead cannot praise, Ps.6.5, Isa.38.18) but the specific transformative moments recorded (deliverance eliciting thanks, sin confessed and forgiven) are treated as decisively completed acts within their narrative -- forgiveness following confession (Ps.32.5, 'you forgave the iniquity of my sin') is presented as irreversible once granted, even though ongoing thanksgiving itself requires continual renewal (life, not death, being the condition for it). | Ps.32.5, Isa.38.18 |
| 3215 | char-answers | draft | Yes, the mechanism differs across contexts: in the cultic/temple context it operates through appointed liturgical office and musical instrumentation (1Chr.16.4, 2Chr.5.13, 1Chr.25.3 -- Levites appointed, with lyre/trumpets); in the personal-devotional context it operates through spontaneous individual speech-vow (Ps.9.1, Ps.86.12 'with my whole heart'); in the penitential context it operates through a required ritual or intercessory-prayer act (Lev.16.21's scapegoat confession, Dan.9.4's extended prayer of confession) -- three distinct mechanisms (institutional/musical, personal/vocal, ritual/legal) all realizing the same root verb. | 1Chr.16.4, Ps.86.12, Lev.16.21 |
| 3224 | char-answers | draft | The characteristic is blocked/negated by death and the underworld: Sheol and the dead cannot thank or praise God (Isa.38.18, Ps.6.5, Ps.30.9, Ps.88.10) -- consciousness and life are the necessary inner-being conditions without which the act cannot occur at all, showing an absolute rather than merely moral block. | Isa.38.18, Ps.6.5 |
| 3226 | char-answers | draft | The evidence (Ps.49.18's ironic 'praise' given to a self-satisfied rich man by flatterers, and Prov.28.13's concealer of transgression) suggests a state of self-satisfaction or denial blocks genuine uptake, but the subgroup's own verses do not directly describe the inner state of a person in whom thanksgiving/confession is present-but-inert (as opposed to simply absent); resolving this fully would need the surrounding verses of Ps.49 and Prov.28 to establish whether the characteristic is present-but-unfruitful or simply not present in these people. | Ps.49.18, Prov.28.13 |
| 3229 | char-answers | draft | The relational scope includes the covenant community (Israel, Levites, kings), the wider nations/peoples (hoped-for or commanded to join), and even hostile or non-agentive subjects rhetorically co-opted (wrath of man, Ps.76.10); explicitly excluded are the dead/Sheol, who structurally cannot participate (Isa.38.18, Ps.6.5, Ps.30.9, Ps.88.10) -- the scope is maximal among the living but categorically bounded by death. | Ps.76.10, Ps.88.10 |
| 3230 | char-answers | draft | The characteristic's absence among the dead and presence only among the living (Ps.6.5, Isa.38.18, Ps.88.10, Ps.30.9) indicates that thanksgiving/praise is bound to the ongoing, conscious life of the human person -- it functions as a mark of the living divine image (which requires breath/consciousness to exercise), not an abstract capacity that persists apart from embodied life. | Ps.6.5, Isa.38.18 |
| 3235 | char-answers | draft | Where the characteristic is present (wholehearted praise, honest confession) it indicates a rightly functioning, responsive image-bearer in right relation to God; where it is absent through concealment of sin (Prov.28.13) it indicates a distorted or self-protective condition of the image; where it is structurally absent through death (Ps.6.5, Isa.38.18) it indicates the image's exercise is contingent on embodied life rather than a permanent indication of the image's condition per se. | Prov.28.13, Ps.6.5 |
| 3244 | char-answers | draft | No occurrence within this subgroup directly depicts a person who is exposed to another's call/model of thanksgiving or confession yet fails to take it up -- Ps.49.18's flattery and Prov.28.13's concealment gesture toward this but describe self-generated postures rather than a response to another's extended characteristic; further analysis across the wider Psalms/Proverbs context would be needed to resolve this fully. | Ps.49.18 |
| 3248 | char-answers | draft | The pattern of engagement (mouth, heart, whole body in prostration) versus non-engagement (never engaging in the dead/Sheol, Ps.6.5/Isa.38.18) indicates the characteristic's nature as a fully embodied, life-dependent act requiring the coordinated engagement of inner faculties (heart's conviction) and outer faculties (voice, posture) together -- it is not a purely cognitive or purely emotional faculty-engagement but demands their union in a living person. | Ps.111.1, Ps.6.5 |
| 3252 | char-answers | draft | At its edge the characteristic resists and excludes self-protective concealment of guilt ('I did not cover my iniquity', Ps.32.5) and false/flattering acclaim directed at unworthy human objects (Ps.49.18's ironic praise of the self-satisfied) -- it excludes both dishonesty about oneself before God and misdirected acknowledgment of a creature as though it merited what only God merits. | Ps.32.5, Ps.49.18 |
| 3476 | verse-reading | draft | In Esth.2.21, 1Sam.24.9, Num.35.23, and 1Kgs.20.7 the surface sense shifts from the base gloss 'seek/desire' toward a conspiratorial/hostile sense 'to plot against, seek to harm/kill' -- a marked negative-valence divergence from the neutral-to-positive base gloss. By contrast in 2Chr.15.4 the surface sense aligns with the base devotional gloss (seeking God). This shows the same lexeme's stepGloss splits sharply by direction of the object across these occurrences. | Esth.2.21, 1Sam.24.9, Num.35.23, 1Kgs.20.7 |
| 3479 | verse-reading | draft | Num.35.23: the operation word 'dropped' (H5307G, role-T3, tagged M02) is directed by the unwitting agent ('he') toward the victim ('him'), resulting in death; H1245's seeking (of harm) is explicitly negated in this verse ('did not seek his harm'), clarifying the dropping was accidental, not an intentional operation flowing from a hostile seeking. | Num.35.23 |
| 3485 | verse-reading | draft | In Num.35.23, M02 ('dropped', tagged as an anger-related code) co-occurs with H1245's negated seeking ('did not seek his harm'); the verse explicitly denies hostile intent (H1245 negated) even though the fatal act (tagged M02) occurred, distinguishing accidental action from malicious pursuit. | Num.35.23 |
| 3578 | verse-reading | draft | In Jer.38.4, darash is used not in its more common cultic/oracular-inquiry sense but in the 'care for, seek with application' sense applied to political/social welfare ('seeking the welfare of this people') -- a civic-care nuance distinct from the term's more frequent devotional usage elsewhere in the OT. | Jer.38.4 |
| 3579 | verse-reading | draft | The operation 'weakening' (H7503, T3/M24) is what the officials (party) accuse the man of doing to the soldiers' hands; H1875 (seeking) is embedded in the officials' claim that he is NOT seeking the people's welfare -- the negated seeking-operation stands opposed to the weakening-operation as the officials' charge against him. | Jer.38.4 |
| 3580 | verse-reading | draft | M33 (welfare, H7965I) and M24/M58 (harm, H7451C) are the direct antithetical objects governed by H1875's negated seeking in this verse -- the officials frame the accused as failing to seek M33 and instead effectively producing M24, making H1875 the pivotal verb linking these two opposed M-codes as alternatives. | Jer.38.4 |
| 3637 | verse-reading | draft | 2Kgs.1.2 'inquire of Baal-zebub' applies darash to a FALSE deity rather than the Lord -- this is within the attested sense range ('of heathen gods, necromancers') but the specific object here (a Philistine idol) carries an ironic/condemnatory nuance: the king seeking illegitimate divine guidance instead of Yahweh, contrasting sharply with the same verb's use for the Lord in 2Kgs.3.11 and 2Kgs.8.8. | 2Kgs.1.2 |
| 3638 | verse-reading | draft | 2Kgs.8.8 'inquire of the Lord through him [the man of God]' matches the base cultic-consultation sense exactly, paralleling 2Kgs.3.11 -- no notable divergence. | 2Kgs.8.8 |
| 3639 | verse-reading | draft | Ps.22.26 'those who seek him' uses darash in its worshipful/devotional sense (seeking God in praise and satisfaction) rather than the oracular-inquiry sense dominant in the Kings narratives -- here the nuance is communal, joyful seeking tied to being satisfied and praising, showing the term's range extending to worship-postures beyond formal consultation. | Ps.22.26 |
| 3641 | verse-reading | draft | 2Kgs.1.2 co-occurs darash with M02 (fell), M03/M21/M73 (sick), M25 (recover), M73 (sickness). From inquiring's vantage: the inquiry is the RESPONSE to the king's affliction -- darash here functions as the action triggered by sickness/fear-of-death, seeking an oracle about recovery, but the sickness-cluster words are the occasion, not something inquiring itself modifies. | 2Kgs.1.2 |
| 3642 | verse-reading | draft | 2Kgs.8.8 co-occurs darash with M72 (king), M36 (present/gift), M37 (meet), M25 (recover), M73 (sickness). From inquiring's vantage: the inquiry-of-the-Lord is again a response to sickness (M73/M25), mediated through a gift (M36) and a meeting (M37) with the man of God -- inquiring is the culminating act the other elements (gift, journey, meeting) serve to enable. | 2Kgs.8.8 |
| 3643 | verse-reading | draft | Ps.22.26 co-occurs darash (seek) with M09 (afflicted), M45 (satisfied), M08/M16/M22 (praise), M47 (hearts), M34 (forever). | 5492 | verse-reading | draft | In 2 Chr 15:13 darash carries its well-attested devotional sense 'seek the Lord' -- this is within the base gloss's own listed sub-sense (1a3, seeking deity in prayer/worship), not a divergence from it; contrast with the necromantic-object occurrences shows the same verb pivoting entirely on its object rather than on any special contextual nuance of the verb form itself. | 2Chr.15.13 |

| 5494 | verse-reading | draft | M57 (breach of faith, H4603/H4604) and M65 (command, H1697G) are present in 1 Chr 10:13 alongside the seeking; the seeking-of-a-medium is presented as the culminating instance/proof of the breach of faith and failure to keep the command -- the seeking characteristic supplies the concrete act that instantiates the abstract unfaithfulness named by the other M-codes. | 1Chr.10.13 |
| 5495 | verse-reading | draft | No other M-code characteristic co-occurs in 2 Chr 15:13 alongside the seeking of the Lord; the other tagged items are structural/role tags only. | 2Chr.15.13 |
| 5496 | verse-reading | draft | No role-T3 operation tag is present in Deut 18:11 co-occurring with a distinct party tag beyond the practitioner terms themselves; the inquiring here is the M83 term's own act, not paired with a separate operation-word directed at another party. | Deut.18.11 |
| 5497 | verse-reading | draft | In 1 Chr 10:13 the operation 'died' (H4191, role-T3) applies to Saul; the seeking (H1875, consulting the medium) is Saul's own initiating act directed toward the medium as the consulted party, which precipitates the death operation upon himself. | 1Chr.10.13 |
| 5498 | verse-reading | draft | In 2 Chr 15:13 the operation 'put to death' (H4191, role-T3) is directed by the covenant community against whoever fails to seek the Lord; the seeking itself (H1875) is the required act of the individual party directed toward the Lord/God of Israel, and its absence triggers the death-operation against that same individual. | 2Chr.15.13 |
| 5727 | verse-reading | draft | Rev.9.6's 'seek death' inverts the term's usual desirous-of-good connotation into an ironic, desperate seeking of one's own demise -- a stark contextual divergence carrying the nuance of unrelieved torment, contrasted against Mark.14.1/Acts.17.5's hostile-seeking-of-another and against the ordinary benign senses in Matt/Luke. | Rev.9.6 |
| 5729 | verse-reading | draft | No other M-code characteristic co-occurs with zeteo in Rev.9.6; the verse's other tagged terms (long/G1937 = M18) belongs to a different word not thematically overlapping as a distinct co-present characteristic beyond the shared death-longing motif. | Rev.9.6 |
| 5757 | verse-reading | draft | In Rev.9.6 zeteo ('seek') is the operation, initiated by the tormented people, directed toward death itself as the sought object -- a human-to-object (self-destructive) directed act. | Rev.9.6 |
| 5791 | verse-reading | draft | In Ezek.20.31 'inquired of' shifts from ordinary consultation to a formal, refused oracular hearing ('I will not be inquired of by you') -- the divergence carries a judgment-nuance of divine withholding not present in the base gloss's neutral 'to consult'; contrasts with 1Chr.28.9 where God's own searching (active, not withheld) inverts the direction of the same root sense. | Ezek.20.31 |
| 5794 | verse-reading | draft | M10c (defile, H2930A) and other M-codes (M19/M50/M56/M60/M70 sacrifice-cluster on H5375H; M02/M08/M56 on H5674A; M42 on H5002; M25/M44 on H2416A) co-occur in Ezek.20.31 with H1875's inquiry, but relate to the idolatrous sacrifice practice that provokes God's refusal to be inquired of -- H1875's own vantage adds that the refusal to answer is itself the covenantal consequence of the defilement, marking seeking as the relational channel that idolatry has severed. | Ezek.20.31 |
| 5799 | verse-reading | draft | H1875 itself is not tagged role-T3 in these verses (its own tag is M83); the T3-tagged operations present (e.g. H5375H offering, H5674A offer up, H2930A defile in Ezek.20.31) are separate strongs from H1875, so no D7.7.1 relation attaches directly to H1875 as the operation-word; rather H1875 is itself the object of divine refusal, not an initiator of a T3-tagged act, in Ezek.20.31. | Ezek.20.31 |
| 6258 | verse-reading | draft | In Rom.2.7 zeteo (kath' hupomonen ergou agathou doxan... zetousin) sits within its base gloss of diligent pursuit/striving toward a good end -- no unusual nuance beyond the standard 'seek/strive for' sense used elsewhere for pursuing eschatological goods. | Rom.2.7 |
| 6259 | verse-reading | draft | The operation word zeteo ('seek') is directed by 'those who' (the subject party, believers described by patient well-doing) toward the object 'glory and honor and immortality' -- the seeking party initiates the action toward the divine reward, not toward another person. | Rom.2.7 |
| 6260 | verse-reading | draft | Seeking (G2212) here functions as the operative disposition that yields glory (M22), honor (M71), and immortality (M61) via well-doing (M05) and patience (M34) and eternal life (M25); seeking is the pursuit-verb binding these other characteristics together as the sought objects/means, giving the first relational read of this verse's M-code cluster. | Rom.2.7 |
| 6431 | verse-reading | draft | In 1Sam.19.10, 1Sam.23.15, and 1Sam.25.26 'sought/seek' carries the ordinary hostile-pursuit sense (seeking to kill/harm), consistent with the verb's attested range and not a special divergence from the base gloss. | 1Sam.19.10, 1Sam.23.15, 1Sam.25.26 |
| 6441 | verse-reading | draft | In 1Sam.25.26 unnamed 'enemies' (agentive party) are the initiators of seeking, directed toward doing evil to 'my lord' (David, T7/T8 party). | 1Sam.25.26 |
| 6451 | verse-reading | draft | In 1Sam.25.26 baqash co-occurs with M25/M44 (lives, oath formula), M47 (soul), M45 (saving), M06 (enemies), M24/M58 (evil); the seeking-to-do-evil is characterized by the enmity (M06) and evil (M24/M58) terms, while the oath-formula and saving/restraining language belong to a separate part of the blessing/curse structure -- baqash relates most directly to M06/M24 as the hostile party's action, but not to the oath or saving terms. | 1Sam.25.26 |
| 6485 | verse-reading | draft | In 1Sam.25.29 baqash carries its base sense of hostile pursuit of a life; the surface form 'seek' sits within the general stepGloss with no additional nuance beyond the standard hunting-for-life sense also seen in 1Sam.26.20. | 1Sam.25.29 |
| 6495 | verse-reading | draft | M47 (life/soul) co-occurs with baqash in 1Sam.25.29 as the object of hostile seeking; the seeking-characteristic here functions as the operative verb directing lethal pursuit toward the 'life' M-code term, but the relation is one of grammatical object, not thematic overlap between the two characteristics themselves. | 1Sam.25.29 |
| 6504 | verse-reading | draft | In 1Sam.25.29 baqash (role-T3, seek) is directed by 'men' who rise to pursue toward the 'life' of the lord (David) -- the seeking is initiated by hostile pursuers and targets the party whose life is bound in God's care. | 1Sam.25.29 |
| 6548 | verse-reading | draft | In Esth.6.2, Job.10.6, Ps.24.6, Ps.27.4, Ps.34.14, Ps.35.4, and Ps.37.36 baqash is used in its standard senses (hostile pursuit, investigative search, devotional seeking, seeking peace) with no notable divergence from the base gloss. | Esth.6.2, Ps.27.4, Ps.34.14, Ps.35.4, Ps.37.36 |
| 6557 | verse-reading | draft | Other M-codes present in Ps.27.4: M21 (asked), M18 (beauty). Baqash (seek) here names the psalmist's total posture toward the Lord's house, which is expressed concretely through asking (M21) and gazing on beauty (M18); baqash functions as the umbrella intention, not a modifier of either. | Ps.27.4 |
| 6569 | verse-reading | draft | In Ps.27.4, H1245 (seek) is an operation initiated by the psalmist, directed toward the Lord — specifically toward dwelling in his house and gazing on his beauty, marking God as the object of pursuit. | Ps.27.4 |
| 6891 | verse-reading | draft | In Gen.9.5 darash is used three times in the specific legal-forensic sense 'require a reckoning/exact an accounting' for bloodshed, sharply narrower than the general 'seek/inquire' gloss; here it carries judicial, retributive force (God as avenger holding to account) rather than devotional or investigative seeking, distinguishing it from occurrences like Ps.34.10 or Heb.11.6 where the devotional sense dominates. | Gen.9.5 |
| 6897 | verse-reading | draft | Gen.9.5: no other M-code characteristic co-occurs in this verse alongside darash ('reckoning'/'require'). | Ps.9.12, Isa.9.13, Gen.9.5 |
| 6902 | verse-reading | draft | Gen.9.5: God (implicit speaker/party) is the initiator of the require-operation, directed toward beast and man/fellow-man as the parties from whom bloodguilt is exacted. | Gen.9.5 |
| 7073 | char-reading | draft | Across this pass's 'inquire' surface-form cluster (1Kgs.22.8, 2Chr.18.7, 2Kgs.22.13/2Chr.34.21, 2Kgs.3.11, 2Kgs.1.2/1.3/1.6/1.16, 2Kgs.8.8, 1Kgs.14.5, Ezek.20.3, 1Sam.28.7, Jer.10.21, Gen.25.22, Exod.18.15, Deut.12.30, 1Sam.9.9, 1Kgs.22.7/2Chr.18.6, 2Kgs.22.18/2Chr.34.26, Isa.8.19, Jer.37.7, 2Chr.32.31, Isa.19.3, Zeph.1.6, Isa.11.10, Ezek.20.1, 1Chr.21.30, Isa.9.13) the term consistently denotes formal consultation of a deity or authoritative source (the Lord, a prophet, an oracle, Baal-zebub, mediums/necromancers) for a verdict or answer -- the single largest and most cohesive sense-cluster in H1875's full occurrence set, matching this subgroup's core description of institutional/cultic resort to authority for guidance. | 1Kgs.22.8, 2Chr.18.7, 2Kgs.22.13, 2Chr.34.21, 2Kgs.3.11, 2Kgs.1.2, 2Kgs.1.3, 2Kgs.1.6 … |
| 7074 | char-reading | draft | The 'Seek' surface form in this pass (Amos.5.4/5.6/5.14, 1Chr.16.11, Ps.105.4, Isa.55.6, Isa.34.16) is used almost exclusively in imperative form as an urgent devotional/covenantal exhortation ('Seek the LORD/his strength/good and live') rather than as narrative report of consultation -- a distinct sub-mode of this subgroup's formal-inquiry sense: exhortation to habitual, life-orienting seeking of God rather than a single act of consulting an oracle for a specific verdict. | Amos.5.4, Amos.5.6, Amos.5.14, 1Chr.16.11, Ps.105.4, Isa.55.6, Isa.34.16 |
| 7075 | char-reading | draft | The 'require' surface form (Deut.18.19, Ezek.34.10, Mic.6.8, Ezek.20.40, Ezek.33.6, Deut.23.21, Gen.9.5) diverges sharply from the consultation sense: here H1875 denotes a superior party (God) formally demanding an accounting, exacting a debt, or requiring conduct/contributions from an inferior party -- the direction of the inquiry is inverted (God is the one requiring, not the one consulted), showing this single root spans both 'humans formally consulting a superior' AND 'a superior formally demanding of an inferior,' unified by the notion of a binding, authoritative claim being pressed rather than a casual search. | Deut.18.19, Ezek.34.10, Mic.6.8, Ezek.20.40, Ezek.33.6, Deut.23.21, Gen.9.5 |
| 7076 | char-reading | draft | The 'inquired'/'seeking'/'searched' occurrences in this pass show the root's outer boundary away from formal cultic consultation: Lev.10.16 (procedural cultic checking of a sacrifice), 2Sam.11.3 (mundane social inquiry about a woman's identity), Jer.38.4/2Chr.31.21 (civic/devotional 'seeking welfare' or 'seeking God' as ongoing disposition), Ps.109.10 (destitute foraging for food), Ezek.34.8 (pastoral searching for lost sheep), and Judg.6.29 (forensic investigation of who committed a deed) -- these occurrences share the base sense of careful, purposeful inquiry but apply it to secular, pastoral, or forensic objects rather than deity/oracle consultation, showing the root's flexibility extends the subgroup's core 'formal consultation' sense outward to any object warranting diligent investigation. | Lev.10.16, 2Sam.11.3, Jer.38.4, 1Chr.10.13, Ps.109.10, 2Chr.31.21, Ezek.34.8, Judg.6.29 |
| 7077 | char-reading | draft | Ezek.20.3's two Niphal ('inquired'/'be inquired of') occurrences and Ezek.20.31's Niphal 'inquired' show a distinct passive/received-voice usage found nowhere else in this pass's occurrence set: God as the grammatical patient who 'allows himself to be inquired of' -- or here, refuses to be. This voice-shift (Qal active subject seeking vs. Niphal passive object being sought) is meaning-relevant: it marks the sole point in the corpus where the deity, not the human party, controls whether the consultative relationship (this subgroup's core dynamic) may even occur at all. | Ezek.20.3, Ezek.20.31 |
| 7080 | char-reading | draft | Isa.8.19's 'Inquire' surfaces as the illicit/idolatrous consultation of mediums and necromancers set in ironic parallel with 'inquire of their God' -- the same verb spans both condemned and legitimate objects of consultation within one verse, a polarity pattern already noted for other H1875 occurrences (e.g. 2Kgs.1.2 vs 2Kgs.3.11) but here compressed into a single verse's rhetorical contrast. | Isa.8.19 |
| 7090 | char-reading | draft | Gen.42.22 and Gen.9.5 (x2) render darash as 'reckoning' via the Niphal/legal-demand sense -- 'there comes a reckoning for his blood' and 'I will require a reckoning...for the life of man' -- a specialized forensic-accountability nuance (blood-guilt exacted) sharply distinct from the consultative/devotional senses dominant elsewhere in this strong's occurrences. | Gen.42.22, Gen.9.5 |
| 7098 | char-reading | draft | 2Chr.24.22's 'avenge' renders darash in its judicial-exaction sense -- the dying Zechariah's plea invokes God 'seeking out'/requiting his bloodguilt, matching the Gen.9.5/Ps.9.12 'avenge blood' pattern of forensic accountability rather than the devotional or consultative senses. | 2Chr.24.22 |
| 7101 | char-reading | draft | Deut.18.11's 'inquires' (participle, 'one who inquires of the dead') names the necromantic-consultation sense of darash -- an illicit person-type usage matching the condemnatory register also seen in Isa.8.19's mediums/necromancers and 1Sam.28.7's medium-consultation. | Deut.18.11 |
| 7276 | char-answers | draft | Several occurrences orient the seeker toward a future promised fullness -- life (Amos.5.4,6,14 'Seek...and live'), finding/being found (Jer.29.13 'You will seek me and find me'), and restored peace/prosperity (2Chr.14.7) -- showing darash is not only a present act but a movement toward an anticipated covenantal reward not yet possessed. | Amos.5.4, Amos.5.6, Amos.5.14, Jer.29.13, 2Chr.14.7 |
| 7280 | char-answers | draft | The mechanism of seeking differs by context: toward God it operates through prayer/worship and prophetic mediation (1Kgs.22.8, 2Kgs.3.11 -- via a prophet); toward false gods it operates through idolatrous consultation or necromancy (1Sam.28.7, 2Kgs.1.2 -- via mediums); toward legal/moral matters it operates through investigation and testimony-checking (Deut.13.14, 17.4, 19.18 -- via diligent inquiry and witnesses). The underlying mechanism shifts between mediated divine consultation, illicit occult consultation, and forensic investigation. | 1Kgs.22.8, 2Kgs.3.11, 1Sam.28.7, 2Kgs.1.2, Deut.13.14, Deut.19.18 |
| 7281 | char-answers | draft | The capacity to inquire/seek belongs to created design -- humanity's original capacity for relationship and dependence on God through consultation (Exod.18.15, Gen.25.22, 1Sam.9.9) -- but its exercise is repeatedly corrupted by the fallen condition, redirected toward false gods, mediums, and necromancers (1Sam.28.7, Deut.18.11, 2Kgs.1.2, Isa.19.3), showing the characteristic itself spans both created design and fallen misdirection depending on its object. | Exod.18.15, Gen.25.22, 1Sam.9.9, 1Sam.28.7, Deut.18.11, 2Kgs.1.2 |
| 7285 | char-answers | draft | The felt quality of seeking varies by context: it is crisis-driven and emotionally anguished in Ps.77.2 and 2Chr.20.3 (trouble, fear), yet presented as a settled, wholehearted covenantal disposition in 1Chr.22.19, 2Chr.15.12, and 2Chr.22.9 ('sought the Lord with all his heart') -- the same term covers both transient emotional response and durable devotional orientation depending on narrative occasion. | Ps.77.2, 2Chr.20.3, 1Chr.22.19, 2Chr.22.9 |
| 7286 | char-answers | draft | The mode of operation varies markedly by context: toward God it operates via prayer, worship, and prophetic mediation (1Kgs.22.8, Ps.77.2); toward idols/mediums it operates via necromancy and consultation of the dead (1Sam.28.7, Deut.18.11, Isa.8.19); in judicial contexts it operates via formal investigation and cross-examination of witnesses (Deut.13.14, 17.4, 19.18, Judg.6.29); and toward abstract objects (justice, welfare, a lost object) it operates as diligent practical pursuit (Isa.1.17, Deut.22.2). The constitutional level engaged also varies -- from whole-hearted devotion (soul/heart level) to procedural legal action (behavioral level). | 1Kgs.22.8, Ps.77.2, 1Sam.28.7, Deut.18.11, Deut.13.14, Judg.6.29, Isa.1.17, Deut.22.2 |
| 7287 | char-answers | draft | Yes, darash regularly operates through speech: it is commanded imperatively ('Inquire first for the word of the Lord', 1Kgs.22.5; 'Seek the Lord and live', Amos.5.4/6/14), addressed as divine self-disclosure ('I will not be inquired of by you', Ezek.20.3), and enacted as spoken request to a prophet or oracle (2Kgs.22.13 'Go, inquire of the Lord for me'). The act of seeking is frequently voiced as command, plea, or reported speech rather than silent internal disposition alone. | 1Kgs.22.5, Amos.5.4, Ezek.20.3, 2Kgs.22.13 |
| 7292 | char-answers | draft | The characteristic clearly crosses covenantal boundaries in the evidence -- it is used both of Israel's covenant-faithful inquiry of the Lord (1Kgs.22.8, 2Chr.15.12) and of pagan/foreign inquiry practices (2Kgs.1.2 inquiring of Baal-zebub; Isa.19.3 Egyptians inquiring of idols and mediums), showing darash is not confined to the covenant community's proper object but names the general human act of formal consultation regardless of which deity or authority is addressed. | 1Kgs.22.8, 2Chr.15.12, 2Kgs.1.2, Isa.19.3 |
| 7293 | char-answers | draft | Relational scope is broad in principle (any nation or person may seek any deity or authority) but narrow in legitimacy: the Lord invites 'a people... their God' (Isa.8.19) and even nations beyond Israel are said to inquire of the root of Jesse (Isa.11.10), showing an eschatological widening of inclusion; yet within Israel's own covenant, failure to seek excludes one from covenant blessing and even life (2Chr.15.13 'whoever would not seek the Lord... should be put to death'), showing exclusion is covenantal-moral rather than ethnic. | Isa.8.19, Isa.11.10, 2Chr.15.13 |
| 7294 | char-answers | draft | The characteristic's presence marks a person as rightly oriented toward God in covenant fidelity, and its absence marks corruption or apostasy (Ps.10.4, 1Chr.10.14 'He did not seek guidance from the Lord... the Lord put him to death'); this indicates that the capacity to seek/inquire is part of humanity's created relational orientation toward God, and its exercise or neglect functions as a diagnostic marker of the divine image's proper functioning or its corruption in the person. | Ps.10.4, 1Chr.10.14 |
| 7296 | char-answers | draft | The human-to-God direction of seeking reveals a relationship of dependence and covenantal trust: the seeker approaches God as the source of guidance, deliverance, and life (Ps.34.4 'I sought the Lord, and he answered me and delivered me'; Amos.5.4 'Seek me and live'), showing the relationship is characterized by the human party's reliance on God's willingness to respond and God's reciprocal availability to be found (Isa.55.6 'while he may be found'). | Ps.34.4, Amos.5.4, Isa.55.6 |
| 7297 | char-answers | draft | Given the characteristic's God-relation (a posture of dependent consultation directed toward God as the source of truth and guidance) and its role (formal, careful inquiry rather than casual search), the aspect of divine likeness it instantiates is relational capacity for communion and consultation -- humanity's created ability to turn toward, address, and depend upon a personal, communicative God, mirroring God's own relational, self-disclosing nature (he 'allows himself to be inquired of', Ezek.20.3). | Ezek.20.3 |
| 7298 | char-answers | draft | The characteristic is an exclusively creaturely analogue rather than a shared attribute: only humans (and other creatures) 'seek/inquire' of God; God himself is never said to darash toward humanity in this evidence (his own use of the root is either refusing/allowing himself to be sought, Ezek.20.3/31, or, in the forensic sense, 'requiring' an account, Gen.9.5) -- the seeking-movement is one-directional, from creature to Creator. | Ezek.20.3, Gen.9.5 |
| 7299 | char-answers | draft | Where seeking is present in a person (2Chr.22.9 'sought the Lord with all his heart'), it indicates the divine image is functioning rightly in relational orientation toward God; where absent (Ps.10.4, 1Chr.10.14), it indicates a corrupted or suppressed image-bearing capacity, evidenced by pride and covenant unfaithfulness leading to death. | 2Chr.22.9, Ps.10.4, 1Chr.10.14 |
| 7304 | char-answers | draft | God's extension of the characteristic -- his readiness to be sought even by those who did not ask (Isa.65.1) and his invitation 'Seek me and live' (Amos.5.4) -- shows a disposition of gracious accessibility and desire for relationship with humanity, contrasted with his refusal to be inquired of by the unrepentant and idolatrous (Ezek.20.3, 20.31), revealing a disposition that is both inviting and holy/discerning. | Isa.65.1, Amos.5.4, Ezek.20.3, Ezek.20.31 |
| 7308 | char-answers | draft | The characteristic is repeatedly shown as a site of adversarial activity: illegitimate seeking is directed toward mediums, necromancers, and familiar spirits (1Sam.28.7, Deut.18.11, Isa.8.19, Isa.19.3) -- these represent occult/adversarial channels that counterfeit legitimate divine consultation, showing darash's object can be corrupted into a vehicle for engaging with adversarial spiritual power rather than God. | 1Sam.28.7, Deut.18.11, Isa.8.19, Isa.19.3 |
| 7310 | char-answers | draft | The origin of the seeking-impulse is multiple and context-dependent: it arises from fear/crisis (2Chr.20.3, sickness in 2Kgs.1.2), from covenantal command (Deut.4.29, 2Chr.15.12), from personal devotion (Ps.119.10 'with my whole heart'), and from national reform initiative (2Chr.34.3, 2Chr.26.5) -- the impulse's source shifts between external threat, internal devotion, and royal/national leadership depending on context, showing no single fixed origin. | 2Chr.20.3, 2Kgs.1.2, Deut.4.29, Ps.119.10, 2Chr.34.3 |
| 7314 | char-answers | draft | The structural opposite of seeking is forsaking/neglecting -- explicitly named as the contrastive pole in 1Chr.28.9 ('if you forsake him, he will cast you off forever') and implied by negation in Ps.10.4 ('does not seek') and 1Chr.10.14 ('did not seek guidance') -- the inner-being reality that excludes seeking is self-sufficient neglect or active abandonment of the source that ought to be consulted. | Ps.10.4, 1Chr.10.14 |
| 7322 | char-answers | draft | Seeking is itself produced by fear/crisis (2Chr.20.3 'Jehoshaphat was afraid and set his face to seek the Lord') and by covenantal command/exhortation (Deut.4.29, Amos.5.4/6/14 imperatives) -- both an emotional trigger and an external directive can generate the act of seeking. | 2Chr.20.3, Amos.5.4 |
| 11762 | strand-reading | draft | Dread and trembling are felt in the heart, the bones, the flesh, the hair and the face. · meaning: Dread and trembling are felt in the heart, the bones, the flesh, the hair and the face. "dread came upon me, and trembling, which made all my bones shake"; "My heart is in anguish within me; the terrors of death have fallen upon me. Fear and trembling come upon me, and horror overwhelms me"; "shuddering seizes my flesh"; "my heart trembles and leaps out of its place"; "My heart staggers; horror has appalled me"; "the hair of their kings bristles with horror; their faces are convulsed" | Job.4.14, Ps.55.4, Ps.55.5, Job.21.6, Job.37.1, Isa.21.4, Ezek.27.35 |
| 11768 | strand-reading | draft | Fear of man set beside trust. · meaning: Fear of man set beside trust. "The fear of man lays a snare, but whoever trusts in the Lord is safe". "you fear continually all the day because of the wrath of the oppressor … And where is the wrath of the oppressor?" Prayed: "preserve my life from dread of the enemy" | Prov.29.25, Isa.51.13, Ps.64.1 |
| 11772 | strand-reading | draft | A trembling heart given in the curse. · meaning: A trembling heart given in the curse. "the Lord will give you there a trembling heart and failing eyes and a languishing soul … Night and day you shall be in dread … In the morning you shall say, 'If only it were evening!' and at evening you shall say, 'If only it were morning!' because of the dread that your heart shall feel, and the sights that your eyes shall see" | Deut.28.65, Deut.28.66, Deut.28.67 |
| 11776 | strand-reading | draft | Fear of a people or a person falls on others. · meaning: Fear of a people or a person falls on others. "many from the peoples of the country declared themselves Jews, for fear of the Jews had fallen on them"; officials "helped the Jews, for the fear of Mordecai had fallen on them". Of Nebuchadnezzar: "because of the greatness that he gave him, all peoples, nations, and languages trembled and feared before him. Whom he would, he killed". Of Ephraim: "When Ephraim spoke, there was trembling; he was exalted in Israel, but he incurred guilt through Baal and died". Of the king of Babylon: "Is this the man who made the earth tremble" | Esth.8.17, Esth.9.2, Esth.9.3, Dan.5.19, Hos.13.1, Isa.14.16 |
| 11792 | strand-reading | draft | A covenant of fear. · meaning: Meaning: the covenant with Levi is named as one of life and peace, and as one of fear. The fear is stated together with true instruction, lips without wrong, walking with God, and turning many from iniquity. Unit 1 (FE-33) already cites Mal 2:5 through the yārēʾ (to fear) words. This hit is the noun in the same verse. Implies: "life" is a life-side word, and it is held. | Mal.2.5 |
| 11795 | strand-reading | draft | Already read through other words. · meaning: Accounted for here. Each verse is already in a ledger through another M01 word in it. | Isa.8.12, Deut.11.25, Gen.9.2 |
| 11796 | strand-reading | draft | Made a horror to the kingdoms of the earth. · meaning: Meaning: the root names the people or land as the thing that horrifies. The feeling is in the one who passes by and hisses. It stands in lists with "a curse", "a taunt", "a hissing", "a reproach". Implies: this horror is public. A people is exposed before others, and the verses give the cause as their own deeds. Jer 34:17 turns the word "liberty" back on those who withheld it. The inner side is small: it is the onlooker's horror, named in another word. | Jer.24.9, Deut.28.25, Jer.19.8, Jer.18.16, Jer.44.22, 2Chr.30.7, Mic.6.16, Jer.34.17 … |
